amortising cache writes into the unit cost.</li>\n            <li>Safety and security as measured properties: refusal correctness, toxicity rate, PII/PHI leakage, injection resistance, tool-permission violations.</li>\n            <li>Multidimensional targets and criterion-referenced thresholds fixed before the run.</li>\n          </ul>\n        </div>\n        <aside>\n          <div><h4>Lab</h4><p>Three requirement statements — support triage bot, contract-clause extractor, research agent — turned into full criteria tables: axis, metric, target, measurement method, owner.</p></div>\n          <div class=\"traps\"><h4>Traps drilled</h4><p><b>Means hide tail latency.</b> Single-axis targets invite regressions on the axis you didn't name. Thresholds set after seeing results aren't criteria.</p></div>\n          <p class=\"quizline\">Quiz: 8 items</p>\n        </aside>\n      </article>\n\n      <article class=\"mod\">\n        <div>\n          <div class=\"mod-head\"><span class=\"code\">M2</span><h3>Eval datasets and harness design</h3><span class=\"dur\">2 h</span></div>\n          <p class=\"ts\">Task statement: design evaluation datasets and test frameworks (part 1 of 2).</p>\n          <ul>\n            <li>Canonical vocabulary — <strong>task, trial, grader, transcript, outcome, evaluation harness, agent scaffold</strong>. Distractors work by swapping transcript for outcome.</li>\n            <li>Start small: <strong>20–50 tasks drawn from real failures</strong> beats a perfect suite that never gets built; scale toward 50–1,000 as effect sizes shrink.</li>\n            <li>Sourcing: production failures, user reports, dev checks, and deliberate edge cases (irrelevant, oversized, ambiguous, adversarial input).</li>\n            <li><strong>Unambiguous tasks</strong> — two domain experts independently reach the same verdict, with a reference solution proving the task is solvable.</li>\n            <li><strong>Balanced sets</strong>: negative cases where the behaviour should not fire; class imbalance turns a useless grader into a 95%-scorer.</li>\n            <li><strong>Harness parity</strong> with production, clean state per trial — shared state produces correlated failures that look like model regressions.</li>\n            <li>Trials per task and model variance; saturation as a signal to harden the suite.</li>\n            <li>Golden set, regression set, exploratory set — three artefacts, three lifecycles, one named owner.</li>\n          </ul>\n        </div>\n        <aside>\n          <div><h4>Lab</h4><p>Build a 25-task eval set from a support-agent transcript corpus: label, balance, write reference solutions, document the harness contract.</p></div>\n          <div class=\"traps\"><h4>Traps drilled</h4><p><b>0% pass@100 on a frontier model means a broken task</b>, not a weak agent. Synthetic-only data misses the distribution that matters. \"Add more cases\" doesn't fix ambiguous ones.</p></div>\n          <p class=\"quizline\">Quiz: 10 items</p>\n        </aside>\n      </article>\n\n      <article class=\"mod\">\n        <div>\n          <div class=\"mod-head\"><span class=\"code\">M3</span><h3>Graders and mixed methodologies</h3><span class=\"dur\">2 h</span></div>\n          <p class=\"ts\">Task statement: design evaluation datasets and test frameworks (part 2 of 2).</p>\n          <ul>\n            <li>Three grader families and their economics — <strong>code-based</strong> (fast, cheap, reproducible, brittle to valid variation), <strong>model-based</strong> (flexible, non-deterministic, needs calibration), <strong>human</strong> (gold standard, expensive, the calibration source).</li>\n            <li>Code-based techniques: exact match, unit and integration tests, static analysis, schema validation, tool-call verification, outcome verification against the environment.</li>\n            <li>Model-based techniques: rubric scoring, natural-language assertions, pairwise comparison, reference-based grading, multi-judge consensus.</li>\n            <li><strong>Trajectory vs outcome</strong> — grade what was produced, not the path taken; the booking agent whose transcript claims success while no reservation row exists.</li>\n            <li>LLM-judge design: one isolated judge per rubric dimension, an \"Unknown\" escape hatch, a grading model separate from the generating model, positional and verbosity bias.</li>\n            <li><strong>Calibration</strong>: validate against a human-labelled golden set before scaling; 75–90% agreement targets; the \"intern test\" — if two humans can't agree 80% of the time, the rubric is too vague to automate.</li>\n            <li>The hybrid norm: verifiable rewards (\"did it solve the problem?\") paired with LLM rubrics (\"is it readable, efficient, secure?\").</li>\n            <li>Partial credit, grading rigidity, and eval cheating caught by transcript review.</li>\n          </ul>\n        </div>\n        <aside>\n          <div><h4>Lab</h4><p>Write and calibrate an LLM judge: draft a three-dimension rubric, hand-label 30 outputs, measure agreement, revise, re-measure, report the delta.</p></div>\n          <div class=\"traps\"><h4>Traps drilled</h4><p><b>The cheapest grader that answers the question wins</b> — \"use an LLM judge\" is wrong when a unit test settles it. One judge grading five dimensions at once. An uncalibrated judge is evidence of nothing.</p></div>\n          <p class=\"quizline\">Quiz: 10 items · Checkpoint A follows (15 items / 28 min)</p>\n        </aside>\n      </article>\n\n      <article class=\"mod\">\n        <div>\n          <div class=\"mod-head\"><span class=\"code\">M4</span><h3>A/B testing and iterative improvement</h3><span class=\"dur\">1.5 h</span></div>\n          <p class=\"ts\">Task statement: conduct A/B testing and iterative improvements.</p>\n          <ul>\n            <li>Offline evals gate; online experiments confirm. Neither substitutes for the other.</li>\n            <li>Prompt and config <strong>versioning</strong> as the precondition for any comparison at all.</li>\n            <li>Regression suites in CI — what blocks a merge versus what only warns.</li>\n            <li>A/B design for LLM systems: unit of randomisation (user, session, or request), sample size and minimum detectable effect, and <strong>guardrail metrics that must not move</strong> — cost, p95 latency, refusal rate.</li>\n            <li>Why per-request randomisation corrupts multi-turn agent experiments.</li>\n            <li>Shadow mode, canary, staged rollout, champion/challenger — what each proves and what each costs.</li>\n            <li>A shipping sequence to reason with: version → regression → offline eval → A/B significance → canary → rollback path.</li>\n            <li>Closing the loop: every production failure becomes a new eval task.</li>\n          </ul>\n        </div>\n        <aside>\n          <div><h4>Lab</h4><p>Given a +3% eval delta over 40 tasks, design the rollout: justify (or reject) an A/B, choose the randomisation unit, set guardrails and stop conditions, write the rollback trigger.</p></div>\n          <div class=\"traps\"><h4>Traps drilled</h4><p><b>A win on a saturated eval is not a win.</b> \"Ship it, the eval improved\" with no guardrail metrics. Comparing a new prompt against a new dataset.</p></div>\n          <p class=\"quizline\">Quiz: 8 items</p>\n        </aside>\n      </article>\n\n      <article class=\"mod\">\n        <div>\n          <div class=\"mod-head\"><span class=\"code\">M5</span><h3>Diagnosis and root-cause analysis</h3><span class=\"dur\">2 h</span></div>\n          <p class=\"ts\">Task statement: diagnose system issues — prompt failure, hallucinations, model mismatch.</p>\n          <ul>\n            <li>The domain's core heuristic: <strong>trace the symptom to the component that changed.</strong></li>\n            <li>A failure taxonomy with the distinguishing evidence for each — prompt failure, ungrounded generation, retrieval/indexing failure, model mismatch, tool and schema failure, context truncation or compaction loss, agent-loop failure.</li>\n            <li>The canonical scenario: <strong>confident-but-wrong RAG answers after a document refresh → investigate retrieval and indexing first</strong>, not the prompt, not the model.</li>\n            <li>Splitting RAG metrics — retrieval recall and precision versus generation faithfulness; one end-to-end accuracy number cannot localise a fault.</li>\n            <li>Ablation as method: change one layer at a time against a fixed eval set.</li>\n            <li>Transcript review as a first-class practice rather than a last resort.</li>\n            <li>Hallucination controls — allow uncertainty, ground in quotes, require citations, verification passes — and what each costs.</li>\n            <li>When the answer genuinely is model mismatch: tier escalation and extended thinking as the cheap fix.</li>\n          </ul>\n        </div>\n        <aside>\n          <div><h4>Lab</h4><p>Five annotated failure transcripts — RAG, agent loop, tool schema, prompt, model tier. Diagnose each, name the evidence, propose the minimal fix.</p></div>\n          <div class=\"traps\"><h4>Traps drilled</h4><p><b>Swapping models to fix a retrieval bug.</b> Adding \"be accurate\" to fix hallucination. Rebuilding a pipeline when one component changed.</p></div>\n          <p class=\"quizline\">Quiz: 10 items</p>\n        </aside>\n      </article>\n\n      <article class=\"mod\">\n        <div>\n          <div class=\"mod-head\"><span class=\"code\">M6</span><h3>Optimising tokens, latency, and cost</h3><span class=\"dur\">2 h</span></div>\n          <p class=\"ts\">Task statement: optimise token usage, latency, and cost-performance trade-offs.</p>\n          <ul>\n            <li>Optimise only once quality is established — the documented order of operations.</li>\n            <li><strong>Model selection and routing</strong>: tier by task, Haiku for speed-critical paths, cascades and escalation, per-stage choice inside a pipeline.</li>\n            <li><strong>Caching economics with the real multipliers</strong> — writes at 1.25× base input (5-minute TTL) or 2× (1-hour), reads at 0.1×; the break-even read count that justifies a write.</li>\n            <li>Cache mechanics that generate items: model-dependent minimum cacheable prefix, four explicit breakpoints, the tools → system → messages hierarchy, breakpoint on the last <em>stable</em> block, and what invalidates a cache.</li>\n            <li>Context reduction: retrieval trimming, compaction, structured over prose, dropping stale tool output.</li>\n            <li>Output-side levers: <code>max_tokens</code>, sentence and paragraph limits rather than word counts, structured output.</li>\n            <li>Throughput levers: batch processing for non-interactive work, parallel tool calls, streaming for perceived latency.</li>\n            <li>Building a defensible cost model — cost per resolved task across a pipeline, and naming what the optimisation gives up.</li>\n          </ul>\n        </div>\n        <aside>\n          <div><h4>Lab</h4><p>Cost-and-latency teardown of a three-stage pipeline: compute cost per task, apply caching and a tier change, recompute, state the accuracy risk taken.</p></div>\n          <div class=\"traps\"><h4>Traps drilled</h4><p><b>Caching a per-request block yields zero hits.</b> Prompts under the model minimum silently don't cache. Downgrading the model before trimming context. Optimising a stage that isn't the cost driver.</p></div>\n          <p class=\"quizline\">Quiz: 10 items · Checkpoint B follows (15 items / 28 min)</p>\n        </aside>\n      </article>\n\n      <article class=\"mod\">\n        <div>\n          <div class=\"mod-head\"><span class=\"code\">M7</span><h3>Monitoring and observability</h3><span class=\"dur\">1.5 h</span></div>\n          <p class=\"ts\">Task statement: monitor system performance using logging and observability tools.</p>\n          <ul>\n            <li>What a Claude system must emit: full traces carrying prompt version, model, tool calls, token counts, cache hit/miss and stop reason — plus latency percentiles, error and refusal rates, cost per session, escalation rate.</li>\n            <li>Traces as the bridge to evals: the log format should make a failed interaction <strong>replayable as a new eval task</strong>.</li>\n            <li>Online evals — sampling live traffic into judges, with sampling rates and cost ceilings.</li>\n            <li>Drift: input distribution, retrieval corpus, model version, eval-set staleness — and which signal catches each.</li>\n            <li>Alerting that survives a pager rotation: thresholds on rates and percentiles, never absolute counts, tied to the same guardrails the A/B used.</li>\n            <li>Dashboards by audience — engineer sees traces, architect sees quality and cost trend, executive sees outcome per dollar.</li>\n            <li>Logging and privacy: PII in prompts, retention windows, redaction before storage — the seam with Governance.</li>\n          </ul>\n        </div>\n        <aside>\n          <div><h4>Lab</h4><p>Write the observability plan for a given architecture: event schema, six dashboard metrics, four alerts with thresholds, sampling policy, retention and redaction rules.</p></div>\n          <div class=\"traps\"><h4>Traps drilled</h4><p><b>Logging outputs without inputs or versions</b> makes diagnosis impossible. Alerting on absolute counts. Sampling only failures. Monitoring the model but not retrieval.</p></div>\n          <p class=\"quizline\">Quiz: 8 items</p>\n        </aside>\n      </article>\n\n      <article class=\"mod\">\n        <div>\n          <div class=\"mod-head\"><span class=\"code\">M8</span><h3>Where eval meets the other domains</h3><span class=\"dur\">1 h</span></div>\n          <p class=\"ts\">Cross-domain reinforcement — no new task statement.</p>\n          <ul>\n            <li><strong>Governance</strong>: safety evals, red-teaming, human-in-the-loop placement justified by measured error severity, evidence packs for compliance review.</li>\n            <li><strong>Integration</strong>: evaluating retrieval and tool layers separately; tool-selection accuracy across MCP servers.</li>\n            <li><strong>Solution design</strong>: using eval results to choose between workflow and agentic patterns.</li>\n            <li><strong>Stakeholder communication</strong>: reporting results honestly — intervals, blind spots, what the number does not cover — and setting SLAs you can actually measure.</li>\n            <li><strong>Developer enablement</strong>: evals in CI and who owns the suite in the team model.</li>\n          </ul>\n        </div>\n        <aside>\n          <div><h4>Lab</h4><p>Write a one-page eval report for an executive sponsor from a supplied results table.</p></div>\n ## 🤖 Claude

Researched the exam, then built the outline. Three files in [/Users/user/eval](.):

- [course-outline.md](course-outline.md) — the course
- [research/exam-blueprint.md](research/exam-blueprint.md) — blueprint facts with per-fact confidence and sources
- [outline.html](outline.html) — published for review/sharing: **https://claude.ai/artifact/D3F26iCcp7zRHgA1a8CWRv**

**What the target actually is.** Eval engineering on CCAR-P is the *Evaluation, Testing & Optimisation* domain — 16% of a 63-item, 120-minute exam, so ~10 scored items, with six published task statements (metrics; datasets and test frameworks with mixed methodologies; A/B testing; root-cause diagnosis; token/latency/cost optimisation; logging and observability). I scoped the course wider than 10 items because the same material decides RAG-quality items in Integration, safety-eval items in Governance, and results-reporting items in Lifecycle.

**Shape of the course.** 9 modules / ~14 hours: orientation, criteria and metrics, dataset and harness design, graders and judge calibration, A/B and rollout, diagnosis, cost/latency optimisation, observability, cross-domain. Each module has a lab and a quiz; two timed checkpoints and a 30-item domain mock. Content conventions match your existing `learn-claude` plugin layout (topic files with task-statement frontmatter + a keyed question bank), so the `quiz` and `mock-exam` skills can consume it unchanged.

Technical substance is anchored on Anthropic's own material — the agent-eval vocabulary (task/trial/grader/transcript/outcome), the 8-step dataset roadmap, pass@k vs pass^k, the three grader families, and real optimisation numbers (cache writes 1.25×/2×, reads 0.1×, model-dependent minimums).

**One caveat worth acting on first.** The domain weights and task statements come from community guides quoting "Exam Guide v1.0" — I could not reach an official Anthropic exam guide document. Sources also disagree on price ($125 vs $175) and on whether Professional items are scenario-framed or standalone. That's why Phase 0 of the build plan is "pull the official guide from Partner Academy and diff it" before any lesson gets written.

Say the word and I'll start on Module 1's lesson, key-facts, and traps sheets — or write a sample question set first if you'd rather validate the item style.

Sources: [Demystifying evals for AI agents](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/demystifying-evals-for-ai-agents) · [Create strong empirical evaluations](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/test-and-evaluate/eval-tool) · [Prompt caching](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-caching) · [Reducing latency](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/test-and-evaluate/strengthen-guardrails/reduce-latency) · [Four role-based certifications](https://claude.com/blog/four-role-based-claude-certifications) · [CCAR-P blueprint](https://claudecertificationguide.com/ccar-p) · [Preporato CCAR-P guide](https://preporato.com/blog/claude-certified-architect-professional-complete-guide-2026) · [FlashGenius CCAR-P guide](https://flashgenius.net/guides/claude-certified-architect-professional-ccar-p-2026-interactive-guide)

```
# SMART Acronym Usage

The page **does not use the acronym "SMART" verbatim**. Instead, it presents the criteria as a bulleted list with the following adjectives:

1. **Specific**
2. **Measurable**
3. **Achievable**
4. **Relevant**

## Exact Quotation

From the "Define your success criteria" section:

> "Good success criteria are:
> 
> * **Specific:** Clearly define what you want to achieve. Instead of "good performance," specify "accurate sentiment classification."
> 
> * **Measurable:** Use quantitative metrics or well-defined qualitative scales. Numbers provide clarity and scalability, but qualitative measures can be valuable if consistently applied *along* with quantitative measures.
> 
> * **Achievable:** Base your targets on industry benchmarks, prior experiments, AI research, or expert knowledge. Your success metrics should not be unrealistic to current frontier model capabilities.
> 
> * **Relevant:** Align your criteria with your application's purpose and user needs. Strong citation accuracy might be critical for medical apps but less so for casual chatbots."

**Note:** This is a 4-point framework (not the traditional 5-point SMART framework which includes "Time-bound"). The page omits the "time-bound" criterion entirely.
```

## 🤖 Claude

Because it's an initialism — Specific, Measurable, Achievable, Relevant, Time-bound — the standard goal-setting framework.

But checking the source, that's a label I imported, not Anthropic's: the [define success criteria](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/test-and-evaluate/define-success) page never uses the word SMART and lists only four properties — "Good success criteria are: **Specific** … **Measurable** … **Achievable** … **Relevant**." No time-bound. So teaching it as "SMART" would attach a label the exam won't use and imply a fifth criterion the docs don't have. Fixing that.

Numbered;\ntopic frontmatter references them by number.\n\n1. **Claims triage assistant** — insurance, high volume (40k/day), cost-sensitive, regulated.\n   Classifies and routes claims; a wrong route delays a payout.\n2. **Contract clause extractor** — legal, batch, precision-critical. Pulls named clause types\n   out of long agreements for review; a missed clause reaches counsel, a false one wastes time.\n3. **Support deflection agent** — multi-turn, latency-sensitive, measured on resolution and CSAT.\n   Answers customer questions and escalates when it cannot.\n4. **Policy knowledge assistant** — internal RAG over HR and compliance documents that are\n   revised quarterly. Answers must be grounded and current.\n5. **Research report agent** — long-horizon, open-ended, expensive. Gathers sources and drafts\n   a briefing; success is coverage, groundedness, and source quality.\n6. **Code migration agent in CI** — verifiable outcomes, gated by a test suite. Ports services\n   to a new framework and opens pull requests.\nEOF\ncat > /Users/user/eval/content/topics/ev-1-01-success-criteria.md <<'EOF'\n---\nid: ev-1-01\ndomain: 4\ndomain-name: Evaluation, Testing & Optimisation\nweight: 16\ntitle: Success Criteria That Hold Up\ntask-statement: \"4.1 Define evaluation metrics (accuracy, latency, cost, safety, security)\"\nscenarios: [1, 3, 5]\n---\n\n## Lesson\n\nEvery item in this domain starts from the same failure: a stakeholder says the system \"feels worse,\" or \"needs to be more accurate,\" and there is no number that settles the argument. Anthropic's documented properties of good success criteria are four — **specific, measurable, achievable, relevant** — and the exam tests each one as a separate failure mode. *Specific* replaces \"good performance\" with \"accurate sentiment classification.\" *Measurable* means a quantitative metric or a well-defined qualitative scale; the docs allow qualitative measures, but only when they are applied consistently and alongside quantitative ones, which in practice means a rubric with fixed levels, not an adjective. *Achievable* means the target is benchmarked against industry results, prior experiments, or demonstrated frontier-model capability — a target of 100% accuracy on an ambiguous classification task is not a stretch goal, it is a broken criterion. *Relevant* means the criterion earns its place in this application: strict citation accuracy is critical for a medical assistant and mostly overhead for a casual chatbot. Note what is absent — the docs never use the acronym SMART, and there is no \"time-bound\" property. Distractors are written from the source's wording, so borrow the source's wording.\n\nThe second half of the topic is that production systems are almost never single-criterion. The documented criteria categories span task fidelity, consistency, relevance and coherence, tone and style, privacy preservation, context utilisation, latency, and price, and a realistic target set names several at once: F1 at or above 0.85, **and** at least 99.5% of outputs non-toxic, **and** 95% of responses under 200 ms, **and** an error profile where 90% of errors cause inconvenience rather than critical failure. That last one is the pattern candidates miss — **error severity is itself a criterion**, not a footnote, because two systems with identical accuracy can have completely different risk profiles. When a stem gives you a business context and asks what to measure, the credited answer usually names more than one axis, and the single-axis option is the trap.\n\nThe third idea is procedural and easy to score: thresholds are **criterion-referenced** — fixed in advance against a standard, never curved to whatever the system happened to produce. A threshold chosen after seeing the results is not a criterion, it is a description. Each criterion also needs a stated measurement method and an owner; \"we'll track accuracy\" with no named metric, no dataset, and nobody responsible is the state the exam expects you to fix. Getting this right is what turns \"the agent feels worse\" into a hill the whole team can climb.\n\n## Key facts\n\n- The four documented properties of good success criteria are specific, measurable, achievable, relevant — four, not five; \"time-bound\" is not among them and the acronym SMART does not appear in the source.\n- Measurable permits well-defined qualitative scales, but only applied consistently and alongside quantitative measures.\n- Achievable means benchmarked against industry benchmarks, prior experiments, or current frontier-model capability — not aspiration.\n- Relevant means tied to this application's purpose: citation accuracy is critical for a medical app, far less so for a casual chatbot.\n- Documented criteria categories: task fidelity, consistency, relevance and coherence, tone and style, privacy preservation, context utilisation, latency, price.\n- Most production use cases require several criteria evaluated simultaneously — a multidimensional target set, e.g. F1 ≥ 0.85 AND ≥ 99.5% non-toxic AND 95% under 200 ms.\n- Error severity is a first-class criterion: \"90% of errors cause inconvenience rather than critical failure\" is a target, not commentary.\n- Thresholds are criterion-referenced — set against a fixed standard before the run, never adjusted to match the observed result.\n- Each criterion carries a metric, a measurement method, a dataset, and a named owner.\n\n## Exam traps\n\n- A target that names one axis only (usually accuracy) when the scenario states a cost, latency, or safety constraint — the credited answer names the constraint the stem planted.\n- \"Time-bound\" offered as one of the properties of good success criteria, or the answer that frames them as the SMART framework — the source lists four properties and never uses that label.\n- Targets like \"100% accuracy\" or \"zero hallucinations\" — unachievable against demonstrated model capability, so the criterion is broken regardless of how important the use case is.\n- Setting the threshold after reviewing the first eval run, or moving it when the system misses — this is the criterion-referenced trap and the option always sounds pragmatic.\n- Qualitative criteria stated as adjectives (\"professional\", \"helpful\", \"concise\") with no scale — measurable requires a defined scale applied consistently, not a better adjective.\nEOF\ncat > /Users/user/eval/content/topics/ev-1-02-accuracy-metrics.md <<'EOF'\n---\nid: ev-1-02\ndomain: 4\ndomain-name: Evaluation, Testing & Optimisation\nweight: 16\ntitle: Choosing an Accuracy Metric\ntask-statement: \"4.1 Define evaluation metrics (accuracy, latency, cost, safety, security)\"\nscenarios: [1, 2, 4, 5]\n---\n\n## Lesson\n\n\"Accuracy\" is not a metric, it is a category, and the exam's accuracy items are nearly always **match the metric to the shape of the output**. Categorical output — a sentiment label, a routing class, an intent — is graded by **exact match** after normalising whitespace and case; reaching for a model-based grader here is over-engineering and the distractor knows it. Free text with a reference answer is graded by overlap: **ROUGE-L** for summarisation, which scores the longest common subsequence between candidate and reference and reports an F1 of precision and recall. Consistency across paraphrased inputs is measured by **cosine similarity** over sentence embeddings (SBERT in the documented example) — a FAQ bot that answers the same question three ways scores poorly without anyone judging whether any single answer was wrong. Subjective attributes — empathy, professionalism, tone — go to an **LLM grader on a fixed ordinal scale (Likert 1–5)**, and yes/no determinations like \"does this response contain PHI\" go to **LLM binary classification**, which catches implicit and paraphrased cases that a regex cannot. In every documented example the grading model is a separate instance from the generating model.\n\nThe second thing tested here is that a single aggregate number hides the failure that matters, and **class imbalance** is the standard vehicle. On a claims corpus where 5% of items are fraud, a model that never predicts fraud scores 95% accurate; the exam expects you to reach for precision, recall, and F1 per class, and then to pick which of precision and recall the business actually needs. Those two pull in opposite directions and the scenario decides: a clause extractor that hands counsel a fabricated non-compete is a precision failure, while one that silently misses a real indemnity clause is a recall failure, and only one of those is discovered downstream. Say which you are optimising and what you are trading away — the credited answer usually states the trade-off explicitly rather than promising both.\n\nThird, design for volume. The documented guidance is to prioritise **volume over per-item polish** — more test cases with automated grading beats a handful of hand-graded ones — with 50 to 1,000 cases depending on the task, and to structure outputs so grading can be automated at all. That structuring decision belongs at design time: a model asked to \"explain its reasoning and give a verdict\" in prose is expensive to grade, while the same model asked for a verdict field plus a reasoning field is gradeable by exact match on one and a rubric on the other. When a stem says grading is too slow or too expensive, the answer is usually to change the output contract, not to hire more reviewers.\n\n## Key facts\n\n- Exact match (with whitespace and case normalisation) is the metric for categorical outputs — classification, routing, intent, structured field values.\n- ROUGE-L compares the longest common subsequence against a reference summary and reports an F1 of its precision and recall; it measures overlap with a reference, not truth.\n- Cosine similarity over sentence embeddings (e.g. SBERT) measures consistency across paraphrased inputs, not correctness.\n- LLM Likert scoring (fixed 1–5 ordinal scale) handles subjective attributes; LLM binary classification handles yes/no determinations such as PHI or policy compliance.\n- Use a different model instance for grading than for generation.\n- Accuracy alone is misleading under class imbalance; report precision, recall, and F1, and read them per class.\n- Precision and recall trade against each other — the business context decides which one the target is set on.\n- Documented eval-design principles: be task-specific, automate grading wherever possible, prioritise volume over per-item polish; 50–1,000 test cases is the working range.\n- Output structure is an eval decision: separating verdict from reasoning makes the verdict machine-gradeable.\n\n## Exam traps\n\n- Reporting overall accuracy on an imbalanced dataset, or an option that \"improves accuracy\" without touching the minority class.\n- ROUGE-L or embedding similarity offered as a check on factual correctness — both measure overlap with a reference, and a fluent fabrication can score well.\n- An LLM judge proposed where exact match or a unit test settles the question — always more expensive and less reproducible than the deterministic option.\n- Grading with the same model instance that produced the output.\n- \"Collect fewer but higher-quality hand-graded examples\" when the documented preference is volume with automated grading.\n- Promising a gain in precision and recall simultaneously with no change to the system — the credited answer names the trade-off.\nEOF\ncat > /Users/user/eval/content/topics/ev-1-03-pass-at-k.md <<'EOF'\n---\nid: ev-1-03\ndomain: 4\ndomain-name: Evaluation, Testing & Optimisation\nweight: 16\ntitle: Consistency Metrics — pass@k and pass^k\ntask-statement: \"4.1 Define evaluation metrics (accuracy, latency, cost, safety, security)\"\nscenarios: [3, 5, 6]\n---\n\n## Lesson\n\nModel outputs vary between runs, so a task is not scored once, it is run several times — each attempt is a **trial** — and the two metrics that summarise those trials answer opposite questions. **pass@k** is the probability that at least one of k attempts succeeds: the metric for \"one success is enough.\" **pass^k** is the probability that *all* k attempts succeed: the metric for \"this has to work every time.\" At k=1 they are the same number. As k rises they diverge sharply — pass@k climbs toward 100% while pass^k falls — which means the same eval run can be reported as a triumph or a failure depending on which one you quote. The exam tests whether you pick the metric from the product requirement rather than from the result.\n\nMapping is the skill. A migration agent that opens a pull request a human reviews and can re-run is a **pass@k** system: one good attempt out of three is a win, and the cost of a retry is a few cents. An unattended agent that issues a refund, sends a customer email, or merges to main is a **pass^k** system: the ninth run that hallucinates an amount is not averaged away by eight good ones, because it already happened. Anything irreversible, anything unsupervised, anything a customer sees without a human in between — consistency is the requirement, so the consistency metric is the one that gates the launch. The corollary is that adding retries improves pass@k and does nothing for pass^k, which is why \"just retry on failure\" is a credited answer in one architecture and a trap in the other.\n\nTwo reporting disciplines come with this. First, **always state k**: a pass rate with no trial count is uninterpretable, and comparisons across different k are meaningless. Second, a **single-trial comparison between two prompts proves nothing** — the difference you are looking at may be run-to-run variance. When a stem shows someone declaring victory after one run each on the old and new prompt, the defect is the experimental design, not the prompt. This is also where consistency as a documented success criterion meets its metric: \"similar responses to similar inputs\" is measured across trials, not within one.\n\n## Key facts\n\n- A trial is one attempt at a task; multiple trials per task absorb run-to-run model variance.\n- pass@k = probability that at least one of k attempts succeeds — use when one success matters and retries are cheap and safe.\n- pass^k = probability that all k attempts succeed — use when consistency is essential: unattended, irreversible, or customer-facing actions.\n- The two are identical at k=1 and diverge as k grows; by k=10 pass@k approaches 100% while pass^k falls sharply.\n- Retries raise pass@k and leave pass^k unchanged.\n- Always report k alongside the score; pass rates at different k are not comparable.\n- Single-trial comparisons between two configurations are indistinguishable from variance.\n- Mature systems need more trials and harder tasks to detect the smaller improvements that remain.\n\n## Exam traps\n\n- pass@k quoted for an unattended or irreversible workflow — the classic wrong-metric item; the consistency requirement demands pass^k.\n- Raising k and reporting the improved pass@k as a quality gain.\n- A pass rate reported with no k, or two results compared that were measured at different k.\n- Declaring a prompt change an improvement from one run of each.\n- \"Add automatic retries\" offered as the fix for an agent that must be right the first time — it moves the metric that is not the requirement.\nEOF\ncat > /Users/user/eval/content/topics/ev-1-04-latency-cost-safety-security.md <<'EOF'\n---\nid: ev-1-04\ndomain: 4\ndomain-name: Evaluation, Testing & Optimisation\nweight: 16\ntitle: Latency, Cost, Safety and Security as Measured Axes\ntask-statement: \"4.1 Define evaluation metrics (accuracy, latency, cost, safety, security)\"\nscenarios: [1, 2, 3, 4]\n---\n\n## Lesson\n\nThe four non-accuracy axes each have a documented way to be measured wrongly, and that is what gets tested. **Latency** splits into *baseline latency* — the time to process the prompt and produce the response — and *time to first token (TTFT)*, the time until the first token arrives. They optimise differently and they matter to different products: TTFT governs perceived responsiveness in a streaming chat interface, while a batch clause extractor cares only about total time per document. Streaming improves perceived latency and changes total latency not at all, which is why \"enable streaming\" is a credited answer for a chat UI and a distractor for a batch pipeline. Express latency targets as **percentiles, never means** — a p50 of 400 ms with a p99 of nine seconds is a system with a visible tail, and the mean hides it. The documented target style is a percentile statement: 95% of responses under 200 ms.\n\n**Cost** is measured per unit of business value — cost per resolved ticket, per processed claim, per merged pull request — not per token or per API call. The reason is that the cheap-looking option often loses: a smaller model that resolves 60% of tickets and escalates the rest can cost more per *resolved* ticket than a larger one that resolves 85%. A defensible cost figure includes everything the task actually consumes: retries, the judge calls in an online eval, cache writes, and the tokens spent by tool results returning into context. Techniques for moving the number belong to the optimisation task statement; here the job is only to define the metric so an optimisation can be judged.\n\n**Safety** and **security** are distinct axes and both are *measured*, not merely reviewed at launch. Safety metrics cover unsafe-output rate, refusal correctness in both directions (over-refusal is a failure too, and one users notice faster), and the error-severity distribution. Security metrics cover prompt-injection resistance, sensitive-data leakage rate, and tool-permission violations — how often an agent attempted an action outside its granted scope. The documented example worth carrying is PHI detection: an LLM binary classifier catches implicit and paraphrased disclosures that a regex or keyword filter misses entirely, which is why rule-based-only leakage checks are a standing distractor. Privacy preservation is one of the documented success-criteria categories in its own right. All four axes then get stated together — that multi-axis target set is what makes a later optimisation defensible, because you can show what did *not* regress when cost came down.\n\n## Key facts\n\n- Baseline latency is total processing time; TTFT is time to the first token — different metrics, different optimisations.\n- Streaming improves perceived latency and TTFT-driven experience; it does not reduce total latency.\n- State latency targets as percentiles (p50/p95/p99), e.g. \"95% of responses under 200 ms\" — means conceal the tail.\n- Cost is measured per unit of business value (per resolved ticket, per processed document), not per token or per call.\n- A complete cost figure includes retries, grader/judge calls, cache writes, and tool results re-entering context.\n- Safety metrics: unsafe-output rate, refusal correctness in both directions, error-severity distribution (e.g. 90% of errors merely inconvenient).\n- Security metrics: prompt-injection resistance, sensitive-data leakage rate, tool-permission violations.\n- LLM binary classification detects implicit or paraphrased PHI/PII that keyword and regex filters miss.\n- Privacy preservation is a documented success-criteria category alongside latency and price.\n- Every axis needs a measurement method and an owner; the multi-axis target set is what makes a later optimisation defensible.\n\n## Exam traps\n\n- A mean or average latency target — the percentile option is the credited one whenever a tail is described.\n- \"Enable streaming to reduce latency\" for a batch or non-interactive workload, where perceived responsiveness is irrelevant.\n- Cost stated per token or per API call, or a model downgrade justified on per-token price while the resolution rate drops.\n- Cost models that omit retries and judge calls, then claim a saving the invoice will not show.\n- Regex or keyword filtering offered as sufficient PII/PHI leakage measurement.\n- Safety treated as a one-time pre-launch review rather than a continuously measured rate.\n- Over-refusal ignored: an option that drives unsafe outputs to zero by refusing broadly still fails the safety criterion.\nEOF\nls -la /Users/user/eval/content/topics && wc -w /Users/user/eval/content/topics/*.md",
```json
  "description": "Write Module 1 topic files"
}
```

## 🤖 Claude

Module 1 is written as four topic files in `content/topics/`, each with the `## Lesson` / `## Key facts` / `## Exam traps` structure the `learn-claude` quiz skill reads:

| File | Covers |
|---|---|
| [ev-1-01-success-criteria.md](content/topics/ev-1-01-success-criteria.md) | The four documented properties, multidimensional targets, error severity as a criterion, criterion-referenced thresholds |
| [ev-1-02-accuracy-metrics.md](content/topics/ev-1-02-accuracy-metrics.md) | Matching metric to output shape; exact match, ROUGE-L, embedding similarity, Likert and binary LLM grading; class imbalance; precision/recall trade |
| [ev-1-03-pass-at-k.md](content/topics/ev-1-03-pass-at-k.md) | pass@k vs pass^k, mapping each to a product requirement, trial discipline |
| [ev-1-04-latency-cost-safety-security.md](content/topics/ev-1-04-latency-cost-safety-security.md) | TTFT vs baseline latency, percentiles, cost per unit of business value, safety and security as measured rates |

Also added [content/scenarios.md](content/scenarios.md) — six fixed business contexts (claims triage, clause extraction, support deflection, policy RAG, research agent, CI migration agent) that later modules and the question bank key to by number, so learners meet a few systems repeatedly instead of a new one per item.

Two things to know about how I wrote them:

**Every factual claim traces to an Anthropic source** — the four criteria properties, the criteria categories, the grading modes, the 50–1,000 case range, the multidimensional target example, pass@k/pass^k, TTFT. Where I extrapolated to make something teachable (which axis a given scenario should optimise, the cost-per-resolved-ticket framing), it's reasoning from those facts rather than sourced claim, so it shouldn't end up in a question's credited answer without checking.

**The frontmatter assumes this is Domain 4** (`domain: 4`, task statement `4.1`), following the one source that numbers it that way. Sources disagree — another orders it third by weight. It's cosmetic until the official guide settles it, but it's in every file, so it's a single sed when Phase 0 confirms.

Next would be the 8 quiz items for M1, which is also the real test of these sheets — if a trap can't be turned into a defensible question with three explained distractors, the trap was too vague. Want me to write those, or move on to M2's lesson?

<details><summary>🔧 Bash</summary>

```json
{
  "command": "cat > /Users/user/eval/content/topics/ev-2-01-eval-vocabulary.md <<'EOF'\n---\nid: ev-2-01\ndomain: 4\ndomain-name: Evaluation, Testing & Optimisation\nweight: 16\ntitle: The Vocabulary of an Eval Run\ntask-statement: \"4.2 Design evaluation datasets and test frameworks using mixed methodologies\"\nscenarios: [3, 5, 6]\n---\n\n## Lesson\n\nThis domain has a precise vocabulary and several distractors are built by swapping two of its terms. A **task** is a single test with defined inputs and success criteria. A **trial** is one attempt at that task; you run several because model outputs vary between runs. A **grader** is the logic that scores the attempt, and one task can carry several graders or assertions — correctness, format, and cost can each be checked separately on the same trial. The **transcript** (or trace) is the complete record of a trial: outputs, tool calls, reasoning, intermediate results. The **outcome** is the final state of the environment. And two different pieces of infrastructure share a similar name: the **evaluation harness** is what runs evals end to end, while the **agent harness** or **scaffold** is the system that gives the model agency — its tools, its loop, its context assembly. The scaffold is the thing under test; the eval harness is the thing doing the testing. An option that proposes \"fixing the harness\" is only credited when the stem makes clear which one it means.\n\nThe distinction that carries the most exam weight is **transcript versus outcome**. A transcript records what the agent said it did; an outcome records what actually happened. The canonical illustration is a booking agent whose transcript reads like a clean success — it called the reservation tool, it reported a confirmation number — while the database contains no reservation. A grader that reads only the transcript passes that trial. A grader that checks the environment fails it, which is the correct result. So outcome verification is the backbone of agent evals, and transcripts are what you read to understand *why* a failure happened: how many turns it burned, which tool it misused, where the reasoning went sideways. Both matter, and they answer different questions — transcripts tell you how the agent works, outcomes tell you what it actually did.\n\nGetting the words right is not pedantry, it is how you parse a stem under time pressure. \"Our eval passes but customers still report failures\" points at a grader reading transcripts instead of outcomes. \"Results are inconsistent between runs\" points at trials and variance. \"The eval agent behaves differently from production\" points at scaffold parity, not at the dataset. Each phrase maps to a different fix, and the exam expects you to route the symptom to the right layer rather than proposing a general improvement.\n\n## Key facts\n\n- Task: one test with defined inputs and success criteria. Trial: one attempt at a task.\n- Multiple trials per task exist to absorb run-to-run variance in model outputs.\n- A single task can carry multiple graders or assertions scoring different properties of the same trial.\n- Transcript (trace): the complete record of a trial — outputs, tool calls, reasoning, intermediate results.\n- Outcome: the final state of the environment, independent of what the agent claimed.\n- Evaluation harness: infrastructure that runs evals end to end. Agent harness / scaffold: the system that gives the model agency — the system under test.\n- Outcome verification is the backbone of agent evals; transcript review explains failures rather than scoring them.\n- An agent can report success in its transcript while the environment shows the action never took effect.\n\n## Exam traps\n\n- A grader that scores the agent's own claim of success — the transcript-versus-outcome swap, and the most common distractor in this module.\n- Options using \"harness\" ambiguously, where one reading means the eval runner and the other means the agent scaffold.\n- Treating transcript review as a scoring mechanism rather than a diagnostic one.\n- Scoring a task from a single trial and treating the result as the task's score.\n- Assuming one task means one grader, so a format failure and a correctness failure cannot be distinguished.\nEOF\ncat > /Users/user/eval/content/topics/ev-2-02-first-dataset.md <<'EOF'\n---\nid: ev-2-02\ndomain: 4\ndomain-name: Evaluation, Testing & Optimisation\nweight: 16\ntitle: Building the First Dataset\ntask-statement: \"4.2 Design evaluation datasets and test frameworks using mixed methodologies\"\nscenarios: [1, 3, 4]\n---\n\n## Lesson\n\nThe documented advice is to start early and start small: **20–50 simple tasks drawn from real failures** is a great start. This lands against a strong instinct to build a comprehensive suite before measuring anything, and the exam rewards the opposite instinct. Early in a system's life the changes you make have large effects, so a small sample is enough to see them; it is the mature system, where the remaining improvements are small, that needs a large, hard dataset to detect anything at all. The documented working range as a system matures is **50 to 1,000 cases** depending on the task. So \"we need several thousand labelled examples before we can evaluate\" is the wrong shape of answer at the start of a project and only becomes right much later.\n\nWhere the cases come from matters more than how many there are. The documented sources are the failures you already have: convert the manual checks developers run by hand into test cases, and turn user-reported failures into tasks, prioritised by impact. That is the fastest route to a dataset that mirrors the real task distribution, which is the first design principle — an eval is task-specific, and a dataset drawn from a generic benchmark measures something adjacent to your product. Alongside the ordinary distribution, deliberately include **edge cases**: irrelevant or nonexistent inputs, overly long inputs, harmful or poor-quality inputs, ambiguous cases where even human consensus is difficult, and the messy surface features of real traffic — sarcasm, typos, mixed sentiment, misleading titles, implicitly sensitive information. A suite made only of clean, representative cases will pass while production fails.\n\nIn practice a team ends up maintaining three sets with different jobs, and mixing them causes trouble. A **golden set** is human-labelled ground truth, small and carefully curated, used to calibrate graders and judges — if you tune a prompt against it you have lost the thing you calibrate with. A **regression set** is the larger automated suite that gates a change, run on every candidate. An **exploratory set** holds the new and hard cases you are actively failing, which graduate into the regression set once the system handles them. Keeping them distinct is what lets you answer two different questions — \"is this change safe to ship\" and \"is the system getting better at the hard part\" — without one contaminating the other.\n\n## Key facts\n\n- Start with 20–50 simple tasks drawn from real failures; large effect sizes early make small samples sufficient.\n- Mature systems need larger and harder evals to detect the smaller improvements that remain; the documented working range is 50–1,000 cases.\n- Prime sources for cases: manual checks developers already run, and user-reported failures, prioritised by impact.\n- Evals are task-specific — the dataset should mirror the real-world task distribution, not a generic benchmark's.\n- Deliberately include edge cases: irrelevant or nonexistent inputs, overly long inputs, harmful or low-quality inputs, and cases where human consensus is hard.\n- Real-traffic surface features belong in the dataset: sarcasm, typos, mixed sentiment, misleading titles, implicitly sensitive information.\n- Course framing (not from the source): keep a golden set for calibration, a regression set for gating changes, and an exploratory set for cases you currently fail.\n- Tuning against the set you calibrate graders on destroys its value as ground truth.\n\n## Exam traps\n\n- Waiting for a large, complete, perfectly labelled dataset before running any eval — the credited answer starts with tens of real failures.\n- A dataset built entirely from synthetic or generated examples, missing the distribution that actually reaches production.\n- A dataset assembled from cases the system already handles, which produces a high score and no signal.\n- Generic public benchmarks offered as a substitute for a task-specific set.\n- Edge cases deferred to \"a later phase\" when the scenario already describes failures on messy input.\n- One combined dataset used for calibration, gating, and development at once.\nEOF\ncat > /Users/user/eval/content/topics/ev-2-03-task-quality.md <<'EOF'\n---\nid: ev-2-03\ndomain: 4\ndomain-name: Evaluation, Testing & Optimisation\nweight: 16\ntitle: Unambiguous, Solvable, Balanced Tasks\ntask-statement: \"4.2 Design evaluation datasets and test frameworks using mixed methodologies\"\nscenarios: [1, 2, 6]\n---\n\n## Lesson\n\nA task is only useful if it has one defensible verdict, and the documented test for that is blunt: **two domain experts, working independently, reach the same pass/fail decision**. If they disagree, the task is ambiguous and every number computed from it is noise — including the numbers you use to justify a model change. Ambiguous specifications are named as the most common defect in eval sets, and the diagnostic is memorable: a frontier model scoring **0% at pass@100** almost always means the task is broken, not that the agent is incapable. The companion discipline is to write a **reference solution** proving the task is solvable as specified. Writing one surfaces the missing constraint, the impossible threshold, or the requirement two people read differently, before the task pollutes a suite.\n\nThe second property is balance. A set of only positive cases — inputs where the behaviour *should* fire — cannot detect the failure mode where the system fires on everything. Include the negative cases: claims that should not be escalated, documents that contain no indemnity clause, questions the assistant should decline. Class imbalance quietly inflates scores in the same way it does in M1's accuracy metrics: on a corpus that is 5% positive, a system that never fires looks excellent, and a system that fires constantly looks fine on the positive subset. The exam's version of this is an option that proposes adding more examples of the behaviour you want, when what the suite is missing is examples of when the behaviour should not occur.\n\nThird, eval sets have bugs, and they fail in a distinctive way: a task with an impossible threshold or a misconfigured success criterion reports failure forever, no matter what you change. When a task never passes across model upgrades, prompt rewrites, and scaffold changes, suspect the task. This is why the fix for a suite that produces unreliable numbers is almost never \"add more tasks\" — volume does not dilute ambiguity, it multiplies it, and a thousand cases with contested verdicts is a thousand arguments. Repair the tasks, then scale.\n\n## Key facts\n\n- The test for an unambiguous task: two domain experts independently reach the same pass/fail verdict.\n- Write a reference solution for each task to prove it is solvable as specified.\n- A frontier model at 0% pass@100 usually indicates a broken or impossible task rather than an incapable agent.\n- Balanced sets include negative cases — scenarios where the behaviour should *not* occur — not only positive ones.\n- Class imbalance inflates aggregate scores and hides the dominant failure mode.\n- Eval bugs are a named pitfall: impossible thresholds and misconfigured success criteria produce permanent, uninformative failure.\n- Adding test cases does not fix ambiguity; it scales it.\n- Ambiguity is discovered by having a second person grade a sample independently, not by re-reading your own criteria.\n\n## Exam traps\n\n- \"Add more test cases\" offered for a suite whose results the team does not trust — the defect is task quality, not volume.\n- Blaming the model for a task nothing has ever passed.\n- A suite of only positive examples, with an option to add still more of them.\n- Aggregate pass rates quoted on an imbalanced set with no per-class breakdown.\n- Resolving expert disagreement by having the more senior expert decide, rather than rewriting the task so the disagreement cannot recur.\n- Treating a task that two reviewers grade differently as a model consistency problem.\nEOF\ncat > /Users/user/eval/content/topics/ev-2-04-harness-parity.md <<'EOF'\n---\nid: ev-2-04\ndomain: 4\ndomain-name: Evaluation, Testing & Optimisation\nweight: 16\ntitle: Harness Parity and Clean State\ntask-statement: \"4.2 Design evaluation datasets and test frameworks using mixed methodologies\"\nscenarios: [4, 5, 6]\n---\n\n## Lesson\n\nAn eval predicts production only if the thing being evaluated is the thing that runs in production. The documented requirement is that **the agent in the eval functions roughly the same as the agent used in production** — same tools, same scaffold, same context assembly, same model and settings. Teams break this for understandable reasons: they stub the slow tool, they drop the retrieval step to make runs cheaper, they evaluate the prompt in isolation because it is easier to iterate on. Each shortcut moves the eval further from the system whose behaviour you are trying to predict, and the result is the scenario the exam likes to describe — a suite that passes consistently while users report failures. When a stem contains that contradiction, check parity before you question the dataset.\n\nThe second requirement is isolation between trials. **Start each trial from a clean environment.** Shared state — leftover files, warm caches, a database row a previous trial created, a session that never closed — produces correlated failures that look exactly like a model regression: several tasks degrade together, the timing matches a deploy, and the obvious conclusion is wrong. This is a named pitfall precisely because the false signal is so convincing. It also cuts the other way: a trial that passes only because a previous trial left the right file in place is a false pass that survives until production, where no previous trial ran.\n\nParity has a version dimension too. Record what produced each result — model version, prompt version, tool definitions, retrieval index snapshot — because an eval is a comparison and a comparison needs both sides pinned. Without that, a score drop after a model upgrade is indistinguishable from a score drop after an index rebuild, and you have lost the ability to attribute the change. That record is also what makes a production failure replayable as a new eval task later, which is the loop M7 closes. The practical tension the exam sometimes surfaces is cost: a full-parity suite with real tools is slow and expensive, so teams run a fast subset per change and the full suite before a release. That is a legitimate design, and it is different from permanently evaluating a stripped-down agent.\n\n## Key facts\n\n- The eval agent must function roughly the same as the production agent: same tools, scaffold, context assembly, model, and settings.\n- Divergence between eval and production scaffolds produces evals that pass while production fails.\n- Start every trial from a clean environment; shared state causes correlated failures unrelated to agent performance.\n- Leftover files, caches, and database rows can also produce false passes that do not reproduce in production.\n- Correlated failures across several tasks at once are a signal to check the harness before concluding the model regressed.\n- Pin and record model version, prompt version, tool definitions, and index snapshot with every result.\n- A fast subset per change plus the full suite before release is a legitimate cost strategy; permanently evaluating a reduced agent is not.\n\n## Exam traps\n\n- Evaluating a prompt in isolation, with tools or retrieval stubbed, and treating the result as a system-level prediction.\n- Concluding \"the model regressed\" from a run where several unrelated tasks failed together — check for state pollution first.\n- Reusing one environment across all trials to save setup time.\n- Comparing this week's results with last month's when neither run pinned a model or prompt version.\n- Adding tasks or trials to make an unstable suite more reliable when the instability comes from the environment.\nEOF\ncat > /Users/user/eval/content/topics/ev-2-05-living-suite.md <<'EOF'\n---\nid: ev-2-05\ndomain: 4\ndomain-name: Evaluation, Testing & Optimisation\nweight: 16\ntitle: Keeping the Suite Alive\ntask-statement: \"4.2 Design evaluation datasets and test frameworks using mixed methodologies\"\nscenarios: [2, 3, 5]\n---\n\n## Lesson\n\nAn eval suite that never changes stops being useful, and the exam tests the two ways that happens. The first is **saturation**: as scores approach 100%, the suite stops producing improvement signal — every candidate change looks equally good, and the team loses the ability to tell progress from noise. A saturated suite is not a victory to protect, it is a prompt to refresh it with harder tasks drawn from what the system still gets wrong. The second is rot: the product changes, the tasks do not, and the suite starts gating on behaviour nobody wants any more. Both are why evaluation suites are described as **living code requiring ongoing iteration**, with **dedicated ownership** — a named owner, not \"the team.\"\n\nThe practice that keeps the suite honest is **reading transcripts regularly**. It verifies that graders are doing what you think they are doing and that failures are fair — a task marked failed because the agent solved the problem in a way the grader did not anticipate is a grader defect, and only transcript review finds it. It is also how grader drift, silent tool errors, and agents exploiting loopholes come to light. The exam treats transcript review as essential practice rather than an optional nicety, and options that replace it with a summary dashboard are not equivalent: aggregate numbers cannot tell you that a pass was earned the wrong way.\n\nFinally, evals are one layer among several. The documented framing is the **Swiss cheese model** — no single evaluation layer catches every issue — and the layers named alongside automated evals are production monitoring, which reveals real behaviour; A/B testing, which validates with real users; user feedback, which surfaces what nobody anticipated; transcript review, which builds intuition; and systematic human studies, which calibrate model graders. The loop closes when production failures become new eval tasks, so the suite grows from incidents rather than from imagination. When a stem asks how to be confident in an agent's quality, the credited answer usually adds a layer rather than perfecting one.\n\n## Key facts\n\n- Saturation: when scores approach 100% the suite yields no improvement signal and should be refreshed with harder tasks.\n- Eval suites are living code — they require ongoing iteration and a named owner.\n- Read transcripts regularly to verify that graders work correctly and that failures are fair.\n- A task failed because the agent found a valid unanticipated solution is a grader defect, discoverable only by reading the transcript.\n- Swiss cheese model: no single evaluation layer catches every issue.\n- The layers that complement automated evals: production monitoring, A/B testing, user feedback, transcript review, systematic human studies.\n- Human studies are what calibrate model-based graders; production monitoring is what reveals real behaviour.\n- Production failures should be converted into new eval tasks, so the suite grows from real incidents.\n\n## Exam traps\n\n- Treating a near-100% score as proof of quality rather than as evidence the suite is saturated.\n- Deleting or disabling persistently failing tasks to restore a clean dashboard.\n- A dashboard or summary report offered as a substitute for reading transcripts.\n- Relying on the automated suite alone, with no production monitoring, user feedback, or human review layer.\n- Assigning suite maintenance to \"the team\" rather than a named owner — the source calls for dedicated ownership.\n- Building new eval tasks from imagined scenarios while a backlog of real production failures goes unconverted.\nEOF\nls /Users/user/eval/content/topics && wc -w /Users/user/eval/content/topics/ev-2-*.md",
```

## 🤖 Claude

Module 2 is written — five topics in `content/topics/`, same Lesson / Key facts / Exam traps structure:

| File | Covers |
|---|---|
| [ev-2-01-eval-vocabulary.md](content/topics/ev-2-01-eval-vocabulary.md) | Task, trial, grader, transcript, outcome; eval harness vs agent scaffold; the transcript-vs-outcome swap |
| [ev-2-02-first-dataset.md](content/topics/ev-2-02-first-dataset.md) | Start at 20–50 real failures, scale to 50–1,000; sourcing from dev checks and user reports; edge cases; golden/regression/exploratory split |
| [ev-2-03-task-quality.md](content/topics/ev-2-03-task-quality.md) | Two-expert test, reference solutions, 0% pass@100 as a broken task, balance and class imbalance, eval bugs |
| [ev-2-04-harness-parity.md](content/topics/ev-2-04-harness-parity.md) | Production parity, clean state per trial, correlated failures that mimic regressions, version pinning |
| [ev-2-05-living-suite.md](content/topics/ev-2-05-living-suite.md) | Saturation, named ownership, transcript review, the Swiss cheese model and the layers around evals |

Two notes on what's in them:

**M2 carries most of the module's exam surface in its traps, not its facts.** The facts here are short and memorable; what separates candidates is routing a symptom to the right layer — "eval passes but users report failures" is a parity problem, "results swing between runs" is trials or state pollution, "we don't trust the numbers" is task ambiguity. The trap lists are written to drill exactly those confusions, and they're the part worth reviewing first if you want to sanity-check my reading of the domain.

**One framing is mine, and I labelled it inline.** The golden / regression / exploratory three-set split in ev-2-02 is standard practice but isn't in Anthropic's material, so the key-facts entry says so explicitly rather than lending it borrowed authority. Everything else in these five files traces to the documented roadmap and pitfalls.

M2 also creates the first real overlap to watch: trials and variance appear in both ev-1-03 (as a metric-reporting discipline) and ev-2-01/ev-2-04 (as a harness property). That's deliberate reinforcement, but when the question bank gets written, those items should be keyed to one topic each so the progress report doesn't double-count the concept.

A regex asked to decide whether a support reply was appropriately empathetic is equally wrong in the other direction.\n\nThe sharper version of that rule is the hybrid norm now standard for agentic work: pair **verifiable rewards** with **LLM rubrics**. methodologies\"\nscenarios: [1, 2, 6]\n---\n\n## Lesson\n\nCode-based grading is where agent evals should start, because the strongest available evidence is usually mechanical: the test suite passed, the row exists, the file changed, the JSON validated against the schema. The technique that distinguishes agent evals from prompt evals is **outcome verification** — querying the environment rather than parsing the response. An agent that reports a completed refund is checked by reading the refund record; a migration agent is checked by running the target project's tests, not by reading its summary of what it changed. Alongside it sits **tool-call verification**: asserting that the right tool was called, with the right arguments, in a defensible order — which catches the agent that produced an acceptable answer through an unacceptable route, such as reading a customer record it had no reason to open.\n\nThe complementary technique is **transcript analysis** — mechanical checks over the trace rather than the environment. These are cheap assertions and they carry the cost and efficiency axes from M1 into the eval itself, which is how a suite detects that a change made the agent correct but three times more expensive. Schema validation belongs in the same family: when output feeds a downstream system, \"parses and conforms\" is a separate assertion from \"is right,\" and separating them tells you whether a failure is a formatting regression or a reasoning one.\n\nThe limitation is the one the family is named for: brittleness. A code-based grader rejects valid variation, and the documented illustration is grading rigidity in its purest form — rejecting `96.12` when the reference said `96.124991…`. The defences are normalisation (whitespace, case, numeric tolerance, key order), asserting on the property rather than the literal, and **partial credit** on multi-component tasks so an agent that completed four of five steps is not scored identically to one that did nothing. Partial credit is documented guidance, and it matters practically: a suite that only reports pass/fail on composite tasks cannot show progress, so improvements look like noise and the team loses its signal.\n\n## Key facts\n\n- Code-based techniques: string and exact match, binary tests, static analysis, schema validation, outcome verification, tool-call verification, transcript analysis.\n- Outcome verification queries the environment for the final state instead of trusting the agent's report.\n- Tool-call verification asserts the right tool, arguments, and sequence — catching right answers reached by unacceptable routes.\n- Transcript analysis supplies mechanical cost and efficiency assertions: turn count, token usage, retries, forbidden-tool checks.\n- Schema validation is a separate assertion from correctness;
